Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 8
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect F from J.

1.  **Identify the local cluster around F:**
    Node F is directly connected to X, K, G, and E.
    Nodes X and K are also connected to F and G. Specifically:
    *   F is connected to X, K, G, E.
    *   X is connected to F, K, G.
    *   K is connected to F, X, G.
    *   G is connected to F, X, K, H.

    This forms a local cluster of nodes {F, X, K, G}. Any path originating from F and heading towards J must leave this cluster.

2.  **Identify the "exit points" from F's cluster:**
    I need to find the edges that connect the cluster {F, X, K, G} to the rest of the graph (which contains J).
    *   Edge (F, E): This edge connects F directly to E, which is outside the {F, X, K, G} cluster and leads towards J.
    *   Edge (G, H): This edge connects G (within the cluster) to H (outside the cluster), which also leads towards J.

    Let's check if there are any other edges leaving this cluster:
    *   (F, X), (F, K), (F, G), (X, K), (X, G), (K, G) are all internal to the {F, X, K, G} cluster.
    *   (F, E) and (G, H) are the only two edges that connect the {F, X, K, G} cluster to any other node in the graph.

3.  **Formulate a cut set based on these exit points:**
    Since all paths from F to J must pass through either the edge (F, E) or the edge (G, H) to leave the {F, X, K, G} cluster, removing these two edges will completely disconnect F from J.

    *   If (F, E) is removed, all paths that start F-E-... are broken.
    *   If (G, H) is removed, all paths that start F-G-H-..., F-X-G-H-..., or F-K-G-H-... are broken.

    Therefore, the set of edges [(F, E), (G, H)] is a valid cut set. The size of this cut set is 2.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Can we disconnect F and J by removing only one edge?
        *   If we remove only (F, E), paths like F-G-H-I-J would still exist.
        *   If we remove only (G, H), paths like F-E-M-J would still exist.
    *   Since removing a single edge does not disconnect F from J, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.
    *   We have found a cut set of size 2, so this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 8
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E from H.

1.  **Identify direct connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to nodes G, I, and B. Any path from E to H must pass through one of these three nodes via the edges (G, H), (I, H), or (B, H).

2.  **Consider isolating H:** If we remove all edges directly connected to H, H will become an isolated node, and no path can exist between E and H. The edges connected to H are (G, H), (I, H), and (B, H). Removing these three edges would disconnect H from the rest of the graph. This gives a potential cut set of size 3: `[(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]`.

3.  **Check for smaller cut sets (size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Size 1:** Can a single edge disconnect E and H?
        *   If we remove (G, H), paths like E-B-H or E-M-J-P-I-H still exist.
        *   If we remove (B, H), paths like E-G-H or E-M-J-P-I-H still exist.
        *   If we remove (I, H), paths like E-G-H or E-B-H still exist.
        *   Similarly, removing any other single edge will leave multiple paths intact. Thus, a cut of size 1 is not possible.

    *   **Size 2:** Can two edges disconnect E and H?
        To determine this, we can look for edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If we find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then at least *k* edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes (by Menger's Theorem).
        Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
        *   **Path 1:** E - G - H
            Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
        *   **Path 2:** E - B - H
            Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
            (Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as (E,B) != (E,G) and (B,H) != (G,H)).
        *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
            Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`
            (Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2. Its edges are distinct from those in Path 1 and Path 2).

    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, it means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

4.  **Conclusion:** We have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths, which implies a minimum cut of at least 3 edges. We also found a cut set of 3 edges `[(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]` that effectively isolates H, thus disconnecting it from E. Since we cannot do it with fewer than 3 edges, and we found a way to do it with 3 edges, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']]}

The target nodes are D and F. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect D from F.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node D (black) is connected to J and P.
    *   Node F (black) is connected to E, B, K, X, and G.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate node D, we must remove all edges directly connected to it. Node D has two incident edges: (D, J) and (D, P). Removing these two edges would completely disconnect D from the rest of the graph, and thus from F. This would require removing 2 edges.
    *   To isolate node F, we must remove all edges directly connected to it. Node F has five incident edges: (F, E), (F, B), (F, K), (F, X), and (F, G). Removing these five edges would disconnect F. This would require removing 5 edges.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges based on isolation:**
    *   Since isolating D requires 2 edges, and isolating F requires 5 edges, the minimum number of edges to disconnect one of the target nodes is 2. This provides an upper bound for the minimum cut.

4.  **Check if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 edge) is possible:**
    *   If only one edge were removed, say (D, J), node D would still be connected to P via (D, P). From P, there are paths to F (e.g., D - P - O - N - B - F). Therefore, removing only one edge is not sufficient to disconnect D from F.
    *   This confirms that the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

5.  **Confirm the minimum cut using edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    *   To further confirm that 2 is the minimum, we can look for edge-disjoint paths between D and F. If we can find two edge-disjoint paths, then at least two edges must be removed to disconnect D and F.
        *   Path 1: D - J - M - L - E - F
            Edges: (D, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F)
        *   Path 2: D - P - O - N - B - F
            Edges: (D, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B), (B, F)
    *   Upon inspection, all edges in Path 1 are distinct from all edges in Path 2. Therefore, there are at least two edge-disjoint paths between D and F. This means that at least 2 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    *   Since we found a way to disconnect D and F by removing 2 edges (isolating D), and we've determined that at least 2 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(D, J), (D, P)]

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 8
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are F and J. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect F and J.

2.  **Analyze Connectivity from F:**
    *   F is directly connected to K, X, G, and E.
    *   All paths from F to J must start by going through one of these four neighbors.

3.  **Categorize Paths based on F's Neighbors:**
    *   **Paths through E:**
        *   F -> E -> M -> J (This is a direct path to J's neighbor M)
        *   F -> E -> B -> ... (These paths then branch through B to H, C, N, and eventually to I, P, O, which connect to J)
    *   **Paths through G:**
        *   F -> G -> H -> ... (These paths then branch through H to I, B, and eventually to I, P, O, which connect to J)
    *   **Paths through X:**
        *   F -> X -> G -> H -> ... (These paths merge with the paths through G)
        *   F -> X -> K -> G -> H -> ... (These paths also merge with the paths through G)
    *   **Paths through K:**
        *   F -> K -> X -> G -> H -> ... (These paths also merge with the paths through G)

4.  **Identify Bottlenecks/Cut Points:**
    *   Notice that all paths from F that do not go through E must eventually pass through G (or X or K, which only lead to G or back to F/X/K).
    *   Therefore, the entire set of paths from F to J can be divided into two main "channels":
        *   Channel 1: Paths starting F -> E.
        *   Channel 2: Paths starting F -> G (which includes paths F -> X -> G and F -> K -> G).

5.  **Test for a Single Node Cut:**
    *   If I remove E: All paths in Channel 1 are broken. However, paths in Channel 2 (e.g., F -> G -> H -> I -> J) still exist. So, removing E alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove G: All paths in Channel 2 are broken (F -> G, F -> X -> G, F -> K -> G). However, paths in Channel 1 (e.g., F -> E -> M -> J) still exist. So, removing G alone is not enough.
    *   Similarly, removing any other single node (like M, H, B, I, P, X, K, C, N, O, D, L, Y) will leave at least one path between F and J. For example, removing M only breaks F -> E -> M -> J, leaving all other paths through E->B and G->H. Removing I only breaks paths directly to J via I, leaving paths via P (e.g., F->E->B->N->O->P->J) and F->E->M->J.

6.  **Test for a Two-Node Cut:**
    *   Based on the observation in step 4, if we remove both E and G:
        *   Removing E will break all paths in Channel 1 (F -> E -> ...).
        *   Removing G will break all paths in Channel 2 (F -> G -> ...).
        *   For paths starting F -> X: X is connected to F, K, G. Since G is removed, X can only go to K (or back to F). From K, K is connected to F, X, G. Since G is removed, K can only go to X (or back to F). This creates a cycle F-X-K-X... that cannot reach J.
        *   For paths starting F -> K: Similar to F -> X, these paths will be confined to the F-K-X cycle and cannot reach J.
    *   Therefore, removing both E and G successfully disconnects F from J. This is a cut set of size 2.

7.  **Confirm Minimum:** Since a single node removal was insufficient, and we found a valid cut set of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, G]

    *   Neighbors of I: {J, C, H, P}
3.  **Look for direct paths or obvious bottlenecks:**
    *   There is a direct path G - H - I. This means H is a critical node for this path.
4.  **Attempt to find node-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove (the minimum vertex cut), we can look for the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between G and I (Menger's Theorem).
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I. (Intermediate node: {H})
    *   **Path 2:** G - K - J - I. (Intermediate nodes: {K, J})
    *   **Path 3:** G - F - M - L - E - B - C - I. (Intermediate nodes: {F, M, L, E, B, C})

5.  **Check for node-disjointness:**
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {H}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {K, J}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {F, M, L, E, B, C}.
    These three sets are mutually disjoint. Therefore, there are at least 3 node-disjoint paths between G and I. This implies that we need to remove at least 3 nodes to disconnect G and I.

6.  **Propose a cut set of size 3:** Based on the identified paths, a potential cut set could be {H, J, B}. Let's verify if removing these three nodes disconnects G from I.

7.  **Verify the proposed cut set {H, J, B}:**
    *   **Paths through H:** Any path using H (e.g., G-H-I, G-H-B-C-I) is broken by removing H.
    *   **Paths through J:** Any path using J (e.g., G-K-J-I, G-F-X-K-J-I, G-K-J-D-P-I) is broken by removing J.
    *   **Paths through B:** Any path using B (e.g., G-F-M-L-E-B-C-I, G-F-M-L-E-B-N-Y-O-P-I) is broken by removing B.

    Now, let's systematically check if any path remains from G to I after removing H, J, and B.
    *   **I's remaining neighbors:** After removing H and J, I can still connect to C and P.
    *   **Can G reach C?**
        *   All paths from G to C (e.g., G-F-M-L-E-B-C-I, G-H-B-C-I, G-K-J-D-O-C-I) must pass through H, J, or B.
        *   Specifically, paths to C from G either go through H (removed), J (removed), or B (removed). For example, C is connected to N and O. N is connected to B (removed) and Y (connected to B, removed). O is connected to D (connected to J, removed) and Y (connected to B, removed). Thus, G cannot reach C.
    *   **Can G reach P?**
        *   All paths from G to P (e.g., G-K-J-D-P-I, G-F-M-L-E-B-N-Y-O-P-I) must pass through H, J, or B.
        *   Specifically, paths to P from G either go through H (removed), J (removed), or B (removed). For example, P is connected to D and O. D is connected to J (removed). O is connected to D (connected to J, removed) and Y (connected to B, removed). Thus, G cannot reach P.

    Since G cannot reach any of I's remaining neighbors (C and P), G is completely disconnected from I.

8.  **Confirm minimality:** We established that at least 3 nodes must be removed. We found a set of 3 nodes that achieves disconnection. Therefore, this is the minimum number of nodes.

9.  **Confirm necessity of each node in the cut set:**
    *   If H is not removed, G-H-I remains.
    *   If J is not removed, G-K-J-I remains.
    *   If B is not removed, G-F-M-L-E-B-C-I remains.
    Each node in {H, J, B} is essential for this specific cut set.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and I, without removing E or I themselves.

First, I identify all possible paths from E to I. I can categorize these paths by the first node they reach from E, and the last node they pass through before reaching I.

Nodes directly connected to E: M, L, F, B.
Nodes directly connected to I: P, C, H.

All paths from E to I must pass through at least one of P, C, or H. Therefore, removing P, C, and H would certainly disconnect E from I. This gives an upper bound of 3 for the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Now, I need to determine if a smaller set (1 or 2 nodes) can achieve disconnection.

**1. Can a single node disconnect E and I?**
Let's test removing each neighbor of E or I:
*   If I remove M: Paths E-M-J-P-I and E-M-J-D-P-I are broken. However, paths through L, F, and B still exist (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-F-K-G-H-I). So, M is not enough.
*   If I remove L: Paths E-L-F-...-H-I are broken. Paths through M, F, and B still exist. So, L is not enough.
*   If I remove F: Paths E-F-...-H-I are broken. Paths through M, L, and B still exist. So, F is not enough.
*   If I remove B: Paths E-B-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-H-I are broken. However, paths through M (E-M-J-P-I) and paths through L/F (E-L/F-...-G-H-I) still exist. So, B is not enough.
*   If I remove P: Paths E-...-P-I are broken. However, paths through C (E-B-C-I) and H (E-B-H-I) still exist. So, P is not enough.
*   If I remove C: Paths E-...-C-I are broken. However, paths through P (E-M-J-P-I) and H (E-B-H-I) still exist. So, C is not enough.
*   If I remove H: Paths E-...-H-I are broken. However, paths through P (E-M-J-P-I) and C (E-B-C-I) still exist. So, H is not enough.

Since removing any single node (including the direct neighbors of E and I, or other central nodes like N, J, G) does not disconnect E and I, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

**2. Can two nodes disconnect E and I?**
To disconnect E and I, we need to break all paths. The paths can be broadly grouped by their final "gateway" to I: P, C, or H.
Let's consider the nodes that lead to P, C, and H:
*   Paths to P: E-M-J-P-I (and D variant), E-B-N-Y-O-P-I
*   Paths to C: E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I
*   Paths to H: E-L/F-X/K-G-H-I, E-B-H-I

Let's try combinations of two nodes:

*   **Removing B and P:**
    *   Removing B breaks all paths through B (to C, H, and P via N).
    *   Removing P breaks all paths through P (from M and from B via N).
    *   Remaining paths: Paths from E through L/F/X/K/G to H (e.g., E-F-K-G-H-I). These paths still connect E to H, and H is connected to I. So, [B, P] is not enough.

*   **Removing B and H:**
    *   Removing B breaks all paths through B (to C, H, and P via N).
    *   Removing H breaks all paths through H (from B and from G).
    *   Remaining paths: Paths from E through M/J/D to P (e.g., E-M-J-P-I). These paths still connect E to P, and P is connected to I. So, [B, H] is not enough.

*   **Removing B and G:**
    *   Removing B breaks all paths through B (to C, H, and P via N).
    *   Removing G breaks all paths through G (to H).
    *   Remaining paths: Paths from E through M/J/D to P (e.g., E-M-J-P-I). These paths still connect E to P, and P is connected to I. So, [B, G] is not enough.

*   **Removing J and G:**
    *   Removing J breaks paths E-M-J-P-I and E-M-J-D-P-I.
    *   Removing G breaks paths E-L/F-X/K-G-H-I.
    *   Remaining paths: Paths through B (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I, E-B-H-I). These paths still connect E to I. So, [J, G] is not enough.

*   **Removing P and C:**
    *   Removing P breaks all paths to I via P.
    *   Removing C breaks all paths to I via C.
    *   Remaining paths: All paths to I via H (e.g., E-B-H-I, E-F-K-G-H-I). So, [P, C] is not enough.

*   **Removing P and H:**
    *   Removing P breaks all paths to I via P.
    *   Removing H breaks all paths to I via H.
    *   Remaining paths: All paths to I via C (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I). So, [P, H] is not enough.

*   **Removing C and H:**
    *   Removing C breaks all paths to I via C.
    *   Removing H breaks all paths to I via H.
    *   Remaining paths: All paths to I via P (e.g., E-M-J-P-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I). So, [C, H] is not enough.

After systematically checking various combinations, it is clear that no set of two nodes can disconnect E and I. This means the minimum number of nodes to remove must be 3.

**3. A cut set of three nodes:**
As established earlier, removing P, C, and H will disconnect I from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from E.
Let's verify this cut set: [P, C, H].
*   All paths to I must pass through P, C, or H.
*   If P is removed, no path can reach I via P.
*   If C is removed, no path can reach I via C.
*   If H is removed, no path can reach I via H.
*   Therefore, no path can exist from E to I.

This set of 3 nodes successfully disconnects E and I. Since we've shown that 1 or 2 nodes are insufficient, 3 is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, C, H]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are B and H.
2.  **Identify Direct Connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to three other nodes: B, G, and I. This means there are three edges incident to H: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
3.  **Consider a Simple Cut Set:** If we remove all edges directly connected to H, H will become an isolated node. In this case, no path can exist between B and H. The set of edges to remove would be [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]. This cut set has a size of 3.
4.  **Determine if a Smaller Cut is Possible (Menger's Theorem):** To prove that 3 is the minimum number of edges, we need to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum cut size is at least 3.
    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        Edges: {(B, H)}
    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H
        Edges: {(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}
    *   **Path 3:** B - E - F - K - G - H
        Edges: {(B, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H)}
5.  **Verify Edge-Disjointness:**
    *   The edge (B, H) is unique to Path 1.
    *   The edges (B, C), (C, I), (I, H) are unique to Path 2 and do not appear in Path 1 or Path 3.
    *   The edges (B, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H) are unique to Path 3 and do not appear in Path 1 or Path 2.
    Since all edges in these three paths are distinct, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.
6.  **Conclusion:** We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H. Therefore, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect B and H. Since we identified a cut set of size 3 ([(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]), this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between M and B.

First, I identify all direct connections from M and B to other nodes:
*   Node M is connected to L, E, and J.
*   Node B is connected to N, C, H, and E.

Next, I systematically identify all possible paths from M to B. I can categorize these paths based on the first node M connects to:

**Paths starting from M through E:**
1.  M - E - B (Direct path)
2.  M - E - F - K - G - H - B (Path through F, K, G, H)
3.  M - L - E - B (Path through L, then E)
4.  M - L - E - F - K - G - H - B (Path through L, then E, F, K, G, H)

**Paths starting from M through J:**
All paths from M through J must eventually pass through node P, as J is connected to P and D, and D is only connected to J and P.
5.  M - J - P - O - N - B (Path through P, O, N)
6.  M - J - P - I - C - B (Path through P, I, C)
7.  M - J - D - P - O - N - B (Path through D, P, O, N)
8.  M - J - D - P - I - C - B (Path through D, P, I, C)
9.  M - J - P - O - Y - N - B (Path through P, O, Y, N)
10. M - J - D - P - O - Y - N - B (Path through D, P, O, Y, N)
11. M - J - P - O - Y - N - C - B (Path through P, O, Y, N, C)
12. M - J - D - P - O - Y - N - C - B (Path through D, P, O, Y, N, C)

Now, I look for a minimal set of edges whose removal would break all these paths.

**Consider a cut of size 1:**
*   If I remove any single edge, there will always be alternative paths. For example, removing (M, E) would break paths 1, 2, 3, 4, but paths 5-12 (through J) would remain. Removing (J, P) would break paths 5-12, but paths 1-4 (through E) would remain. Therefore, a cut of size 1 is not possible.

**Consider a cut of size 2:**
Let's try to find two critical edges that, when removed, disconnect M from B.

1.  **To cut all paths through E (and L, which relies on E):**
    The most direct way to cut all paths originating from M and going through E is to remove the edge (M, E). This single edge removal breaks paths 1, 2, 3, and 4.

2.  **To cut all paths through J (and D, which relies on J and P):**
    All paths originating from M and going through J (paths 5-12) must pass through node P. Specifically, they all use the edge (J, P) or (D, P) after M-J or M-J-D. Since D is only connected to J and P, any path M-J-D-P... is also covered by cutting (J, P). Therefore, removing the edge (J, P) will break all paths 5-12.

**Proposed Cut Set:** [(M, E), (J, P)]

**Verification:**
*   Removing (M, E) disconnects M from E. Since L's only connection to the "B-side" is through E (L-E), this also effectively disconnects M from any path that would go M-L-E...
*   Removing (J, P) disconnects J from P. Since D's only connection to the "B-side" is through P (D-P) and J (D-J), and J's only connection to the "B-side" is through P (J-P), this effectively disconnects M from any path that would go M-J-P... or M-J-D-P...

With both (M, E) and (J, P) removed, M has no remaining paths to B.
This cut set has a size of 2. Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, 2 is the minimum.

An alternative minimal cut set of size 2 would be [(M, E), (M, J)], which isolates M by removing all its direct connections to the rest of the graph except L, which then relies on E. However, (M, E) already covers the E branch, and (M, J) covers the J branch.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(M, E), (J, P)]

    *   M is connected to L, E, F, J.

    *   **Paths via L:**

        *   M-J-P-O-N-B
        *   M-J-P-I-H-B
        *   M-J-P-I-C-B

    We look for edge-disjoint paths between M and B.
    *   Path 1: M - E - B (Edges: (M,E), (E,B))
    *   Path 2: M - F - G - B (Edges: (M,F), (F,G), (G,B))
    *   Path 3: M - J - I - C - B (Edges: (M,J), (J,I), (I,C), (C,B))

    These three paths are edge-disjoint (they do not share any common edges). According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect M and B is at least the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 3 in this case.

3.  **Search for a cut set of size 3:**

        *   M-L-E-B is cut.
        *   M-E-B is cut.
    *   **(G, B)**: This edge cuts all paths that connect to B via G.
        *   M-E-G-B is cut.
        *   M-F-G-B is cut.
        *   M-F-X-K-G-B is cut.
        *   M-F-K-G-B is cut.
    *   **(M, J)**: This edge cuts all paths originating from M that go through J.
        *   M-J-I-H-B is cut.
        *   M-J-I-C-B is cut.
        *   M-J-P-O-C-B is cut.
        *   M-J-P-O-N-B is cut.
        *   M-J-P-I-H-B is cut.
        *   M-J-P-I-C-B is cut.

    By removing these three edges [(E, B), (G, B), (M, J)], all identified paths from M to B are broken.

4.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   If (E, B) is not removed, the path M-E-B (and M-L-E-B) would still exist.
    *   If (G, B) is not removed, the path M-F-G-B (and others through G) would still exist.
    *   If (M, J) is not removed, the path M-J-I-H-B (and others through J) would still exist.

1.  **Identify Target Nodes**: The two black nodes are B and M. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect B from M. I cannot remove B or M.

2.  **Analyze Connections to M**: Node M is directly connected to nodes E, L, and J. This means any path from B to M must pass through at least one of these three nodes (E, L, or J).

3.  **Explore Single Node Removals**:
    *   If I remove **E**: Paths like B-H-I-J-M still exist.
    *   If I remove **L**: Paths like B-H-E-M and B-H-I-J-M still exist.

    Since removing any single node does not disconnect B from M, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

    *   **Consider removing E and J**:
        *   If **E** is removed: All paths that previously went through E (e.g., B-H-E-M, B-H-E-L-M) are broken. After removing E, node L is only connected to M (since its other connection was to E). This means L can no longer be reached from B. Therefore, all remaining paths from B to M must now pass through J.
        *   If **J** is then also removed: All paths that previously went through J (e.g., B-H-I-J-M, B-C-I-J-M, B-N-O-P-J-M, etc.) are broken.
        *   With both E and J removed, M is completely isolated from B, as its only remaining connection (L) is also isolated from B.
        *   This set {E, J} successfully disconnects B from M.

5.  **Verify Minimality of {E, J}**:
    *   If only E is removed, paths through J (e.g., B-H-I-J-M) still connect B and M.
    *   If only J is removed, paths through E (e.g., B-H-E-M) still connect B and M.
    *   Since both E and J are necessary to achieve disconnection, {E, J} is a minimal cut set of size 2.

6.  **Consider other cut sets of size 2 (optional, for thoroughness)**:

No. All paths from B to E or L would need to go through H (which is removed) or through I/P/O/C/N, none of which connect to E or L without H. So {H, J} is also a valid cut set of size 2.
    *   **{I, E}**: If I and E are removed, paths like B-C-O-P-J-M still exist. Not a cut set.
    *   **{P, E}**: If P and E are removed, paths like B-H-I-J-M still exist. Not a cut set.

Since we found a cut set of size 2, and we established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

    *   E - M - J - P - I
    *   E - L - M - J - I
    *   E - L - M - J - P - I

To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for node-disjoint paths between E and I. If I can find 'k' node-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' nodes must be removed to disconnect E and I (by Menger's Theorem).

Let's identify three node-disjoint paths:

*   **Path 1:** E - B - C - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
*   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, G, H}

    *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J}

*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {F, G, H}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {M, J}.

These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. Therefore, there are at least three node-disjoint paths between E and I. This implies that a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E and I.

*   If B is removed, all paths starting E -> B are broken.
*   If F is removed, all paths starting E -> F are broken.
*   If M is removed, all paths starting E -> M (and E -> L -> M) are broken.

Since B, F, and M are the only nodes directly connected to E that lead to I (L only leads to M), removing these three nodes will sever all connections from E to I.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and one such cut set is [B, F, M].

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect N and H. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (edge-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

I will identify edge-disjoint paths between N and H.

1.  **Path 1 (P1): N - B - H**
    Edges: (N, B), (B, H)

2.  **Path 2 (P2): N - O - C - I - H**
    Edges: (N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)
    Let's check if P2 is edge-disjoint from P1.
    P1 edges: {(N, B), (B, H)}
    P2 edges: {(N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)}
    There are no common edges, so P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.

3.  **Path 3 (P3): N - Y - O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - X - K - G - H**
    Let's check if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.
    P1 edges: {(N, B), (B, H)}
    P2 edges: {(N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)}
    P3 edges: {(N, Y), (Y, O), (O, P), (P, D), (D, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, K), (K, G), (G, H)}

    -   Comparing P3 with P1: No common edges.
    -   Comparing P3 with P2: No common edges. (Note: P3 uses (Y, O) and (O, P), while P2 uses (N, O) and (O, C). Node O is shared, but the edges connected to O are different for P2 and P3).

Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between N and H, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect N and H is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all its incident edges. Node H has three incident edges: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

Let's verify this cut set:
*   Removing (B, H) breaks Path 1 (N - B - H).
*   Removing (G, H) breaks Path 3 (N - Y - O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - X - K - G - H).
*   Removing (I, H) breaks Path 2 (N - O - C - I - H).

After removing these three edges, node H is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, meaning no path can exist from N to H. This confirms that [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)] is a valid cut set of size 3.

Since we established that the minimum cut is at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are H and E. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and E.

First, I identify the direct neighbors of H: G, I, C.
Next, I identify the direct neighbors of E: P, L, M.

All paths from H to E must pass through one of H's neighbors and one of E's neighbors.

Let's analyze the paths from H to E by considering the nodes directly connected to E: P, L, M.

**1. Paths through P to E:**
Many paths from H lead to P. Examples include:
*   H-G-F-P-E
*   H-I-P-E
*   H-I-J-P-E
*   H-C-N-O-P-E
*   H-C-B-O-P-E
Removing node P would sever all these paths that go through P to reach E.

**2. Paths through L to E:**
Node L is only connected to E and M. Therefore, any path from H to L (and then to E) must pass through M. So, H -> ... -> M -> L -> E.

**3. Paths through M to E:**
Node M is connected to E, L, and J. Any path from H to M (that doesn't come from E or L) must pass through J. So, H -> ... -> J -> M -> E.

Combining these observations:
Any path from H to E must either go through node P, OR it must go through node J (to reach M, and then E or L).

Let's test if removing a single node is sufficient:
*   If I remove P: Paths like H-I-J-M-E (and H-I-J-M-L-E) would still exist. So, P alone is not enough.
*   If I remove J: Paths like H-I-P-E (and H-G-F-P-E, etc.) would still exist. So, J alone is not enough.
*   If I remove M: Paths like H-I-P-E (and H-G-F-P-E, etc.) would still exist. So, M alone is not enough.
*   Removing any other single node (G, I, C, F, K, X, N, O, Y, B, L, D) would also leave multiple paths intact, as they are not central to all connections between H and E.

Since a single node removal is not sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

Now, let's consider removing two nodes based on our analysis:
To break all paths, we need to sever the paths going through P AND the paths going through J-M.

**Option 1: Remove P and J.**
1.  Removing P breaks all paths that go through P to reach E (e.g., H-G-F-P-E, H-I-P-E).
2.  After P is removed, any remaining path to E must go through M (or L, which is connected to M).
3.  M is connected to E, L, J. If J is also removed, M is only connected to E and L.
4.  H cannot reach M (or L) without passing through J (which is removed) or P (which is removed).
5.  Therefore, removing P and J successfully disconnects H and E.

**Option 2: Remove P and M.**
1.  Removing P breaks all paths that go through P to reach E.
2.  After P is removed, any remaining path to E must go through M (or L, which is connected to M).
3.  If M is also removed, E is only connected to L.
4.  L is connected to E and M. Since M is removed, L is only connected to E.
5.  H cannot reach L (or M) without passing through M (which is removed) or P (which is removed).
6.  Therefore, removing P and M successfully disconnects H and E.

Both [P, J] and [P, M] are valid cut sets of size 2. Since we have established that a single node removal is not sufficient, 2 is the minimum number of nodes.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [P, J]

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Based on the identified path groups, I can select one node from each "bottleneck" point to disconnect all paths.

1.  **Identify direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to G, I, and B.
    *   Node N is connected to C, O, and B.

        Edges: (H, B), (B, N)

        Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)

        Edges: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)
        (Check for disjointness with Path 1 and Path 2: All edges are distinct from Path 1 and Path 2.)

3.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**

    *   (N, O)
    *   (N, B)

    *   If (N, O) is removed, any path using O to reach N (e.g., H-I-P-O-N, H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N) is broken at N.
    *   If (N, B) is removed, any path using B to reach N (e.g., H-B-N, H-G-F-E-B-N) is broken at N.

    By removing these three edges, node N becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore, no path can exist between H and N. This confirms that the cut set [(N, C), (N, O), (N, B)] successfully disconnects the two black nodes.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 8
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from H.

1.  **Identify all direct and indirect paths between B and H.**
    *   **Direct path:** B - H
    *   **Paths through G (neighbor of H):**
        *   B - E - F - G - H
        *   B - E - M - F - G - H
    *   **Paths through I (neighbor of H):**
        *   B - C - I - H
        *   B - N - O - P - I - H
        *   B - N - Y - O - P - I - H

2.  **Consider the edges directly connected to H.**
    Node H has three incident edges: (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I).
    If we remove all three of these edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. This would ensure no path exists between B and H.
    This cut set is `[(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]`, and its size is 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible.**
    *   **Can we disconnect B and H by removing only 1 edge?**
        No, because there are multiple distinct paths between B and H. For example, removing (B, H) still leaves paths through G and I.
    *   **Can we disconnect B and H by removing only 2 edges?**
        Let's assume we remove two edges, `e1` and `e2`.
        *   **Case A: One of the edges is (B, H).**
            Let `e1 = (B, H)`. Now we need to find a single edge `e2` that can cut *all* remaining paths from B to H.
            The remaining paths are:
            *   Paths through G: B - E - F - G - H and B - E - M - F - G - H
            *   Paths through I: B - C - I - H, B - N - O - P - I - H, and B - N - Y - O - P - I - H
            If `e2 = (G, H)`, it cuts the paths through G, but paths through I (e.g., B-C-I-H) remain.
            If `e2 = (I, H)`, it cuts the paths through I, but paths through G (e.g., B-E-F-G-H) remain.
            If `e2` is any other edge (e.g., (F, G), (C, I), (P, I), (B, E), (B, C), (B, N)), it will only cut a subset of the remaining paths, leaving others intact.
            Therefore, a single `e2` is not sufficient to cut all remaining paths. So, a cut of size 2 where one edge is (B, H) is not possible.
        *   **Case B: Neither `e1` nor `e2` is (B, H).**
            If the direct edge (B, H) is not removed, then B and H remain connected. This case is impossible for disconnection.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum size.**

    As we found a valid cut set of size 3 `[(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]`, this is indeed the minimum.

Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and J.

1.  **Identify direct connections of target nodes:**
    *   Node J is directly connected to M, D, and P via edges (J, M), (J, D), and (J, P).
    *   Node E is directly connected to L, M, F, and B via edges (E, L), (E, M), (E, F), and (E, B).

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate J, I would need to remove all edges connected to it: (J, M), (J, D), and (J, P). This would be a cut set of size 3.
    *   To isolate E, I would need to remove all edges connected to it: (E, L), (E, M), (E, F), and (E, B). This would be a cut set of size 4.
    *   Since 3 is less than 4, a cut set of size 3 is a strong candidate for the minimum.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (s-t edge-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, then the minimum cut must be at least 3.

4.  **Find edge-disjoint paths from E to J:**
    Let's try to find three paths that do not share any common edges.
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)
        *   This path does not share any edges with Path 1.
    *   **Path 3:** E - F - X - G - H - I - P - D - J
        *   Edges: (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D), (D, J)
        *   This path does not share any edges with Path 1 or Path 2. (Specifically, it uses (D,J) while Path 2 uses (P,J) and Path 1 uses (M,J). It uses (I,P) while Path 2 uses (O,P). It uses (E,F) while Path 1 uses (E,M) and Path 2 uses (E,B). All other edges are also distinct.)

5.  **Conclusion from Menger's Theorem:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3.

6.  **Verify the candidate cut set:**
    The cut set `[(M, J), (D, J), (P, J)]` consists of 3 edges.
    *   Removing (M, J) breaks Path 1 (E - M - J).
    *   Removing (P, J) breaks Path 2 (E - B - C - O - P - J).
    *   Removing (D, J) breaks Path 3 (E - F - X - G - H - I - P - D - J).
    Furthermore, by removing these three edges, node J becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Therefore, no path can exist between E and J.

7.  **Final determination:**
    Since the minimum cut is at least 3 (from Menger's Theorem) and we found a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects E and J, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (D, J), (P, J)]

Neighbors of E: G, H, L, M
Neighbors of J: M, I, P, D

Next, I will identify all possible paths between E and J. It's helpful to categorize them by the first node encountered from E, or the last node before J.

Paths from E to J:
1.  **Direct path through M:** E - M - J
2.  **Paths through L then M:** E - L - M - J
3.  **Paths through H:**
    *   E - H - I - J
    *   E - H - I - P - J
    *   E - H - B - C - I - J
    *   E - H - B - C - P - J
    *   E - H - B - N - O - P - J
    *   E - H - B - N - Y - O - P - J

I will now attempt to find a minimal cut set of edges.

If I remove any single edge, will E and J be disconnected?
*   If (E, M) is removed, paths like E-H-I-J still exist.
*   If (J, M) is removed, paths like E-H-I-J still exist.
*   If (E, H) is removed, paths like E-M-J still exist.
*   If (J, I) is removed, paths like E-M-J still exist.
Since removing any single edge leaves at least one path, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

**Attempt 2: Cut set of size 2.**
I need to find two edges whose removal breaks all paths. A good strategy is to identify "bottleneck" nodes or edges that all paths must traverse.
From the list of paths, it's clear that all paths from E to J must pass through either node M or node H (or nodes G or L, which then lead to H or M respectively).

Let's consider cutting one edge that handles paths through M, and one edge that handles paths through H.

Consider the edges: **(E, H)** and **(J, M)**.

1.  **Remove (E, H):**
    *   This directly breaks all paths that start with E-H-...-J (e.g., E-H-I-J, E-H-B-C-P-J, etc.).
    *   It also breaks paths that go through G then H (e.g., E-G-H-I-J), because E can no longer reach H.

2.  **Remove (J, M):**
    *   This directly breaks all paths that end with ...-M-J (e.g., E-M-J, E-L-M-J).

Now, let's verify if any path remains between E and J after removing (E, H) and (J, M):
*   **From E, what nodes can still be reached?** E is still connected to G, L, and M. (E, H) is removed.
*   **Can E reach J via G?** E-G. From G, the only way to reach the J-side of the graph is via H (G-H). However, since (E, H) is removed, E cannot reach H directly, nor can it reach H via G (as E-G-H would require E to reach H). So, no paths through G to J.
*   **Can E reach J via L?** E-L. From L, the only connection is to M (L-M). So, E-L-M. From M, the only way to reach J is via (M, J). However, (J, M) is removed. So, E cannot reach J via L-M.
*   **Can E reach J via M?** E-M. From M, the only way to reach J is via (M, J). However, (J, M) is removed. So, E cannot reach J via M.

Since all paths are broken, the set of edges [(E, H), (J, M)] successfully disconnects E from J. This is a cut set of size 2.

Since we established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible, and we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, H), (J, M)]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are O and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove (excluding O and H) to disconnect them.

I will identify all possible paths from O to H and then look for a minimum set of nodes whose removal would break all these paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

Let's find vertex-disjoint paths from O to H:

1.  **Path 1:** O - N - B - H
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding O and H): {N, B}

2.  **Path 2:** O - C - I - H
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding O and H): {C, I}

3.  **Path 3:** O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G - H
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding O and H): {P, J, M, E, F, K, G}

Let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint (meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes):
*   Nodes from Path 1: {N, B}
*   Nodes from Path 2: {C, I}
*   Nodes from Path 3: {P, J, M, E, F, K, G}

These three sets of nodes are indeed disjoint. This means we have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between O and H.
According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect O and H is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths, which is 3.

Now, I need to identify a cut set of 3 nodes. A cut set can be formed by picking one node from each of these vertex-disjoint paths (excluding O and H).

Let's choose the nodes that are direct neighbors of H for simplicity, as they are critical for many paths. The neighbors of H are B, I, G.
*   B is on Path 1 (O - N - B - H).
*   I is on Path 2 (O - C - I - H).
*   G is on Path 3 (O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G - H).

Let's verify if removing the set [B, I, G] disconnects O and H:
*   If B is removed, all paths going through B (e.g., O-N-B-H, O-C-B-H, O-P-J-M-E-B-H) are broken.
*   If I is removed, all paths going through I (e.g., O-C-I-H, O-P-I-H) are broken.
*   If G is removed, all paths going through G (e.g., O-P-J-M-E-F-K-G-H, O-P-J-M-L-E-F-K-G-H) are broken.

Since B, I, and G are the only direct neighbors of H, removing them completely isolates H from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from O.
Therefore, [B, I, G] is a valid cut set of size 3. Since we established that 3 is the minimum number of nodes, this is a correct solution.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, G]

    *   If I remove the edge (L, E), any path starting with L-E-... is broken.

    Every single path originating from L must begin with either the edge (L, M) or the edge (L, E). Therefore, if both (L, M) and (L, E) are removed, node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. If L is isolated, no path can exist between L and J. This means that the set of edges [(L, M), (L, E)] is a valid cut set. The size of this cut set is 2.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm that 2 is the *minimum* number of edges, I need to check if removing only one edge can disconnect L and J.
    *   If I remove only (L, M): Node L is still connected to E. For example, a path like L-E-B-H-I-J still exists. Thus, L and J are still connected.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are J and F. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect J from F. I cannot remove J or F.

First, I will identify all possible paths from J to F.
The graph can be broadly divided into a "left" side (around J) and a "right" side (around F).
Nodes directly connected to J are D, P, M, I.
Nodes directly connected to F are E, X, K.

Let's analyze the paths from J to F by considering the "gateways" to F's immediate neighborhood. All paths to F must pass through E, X, or K.

**Paths from J to F can be grouped into two main categories based on their initial approach to the F-side:**

**Category 1: Paths through node E**
These paths eventually reach E, which is directly connected to F.
Examples:
*   J -> M -> E -> F
*   J -> M -> L -> E -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> E -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> O -> C -> B -> E -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> O -> C -> I -> H -> B -> E -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> I -> C -> B -> E -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> I -> H -> B -> E -> F
(and similar paths starting from J -> P or J -> I, skipping D)

**Category 2: Paths through nodes X or K**
These paths eventually reach X or K, which are directly connected to F.
Examples:
*   J -> D -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> O -> C -> B -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> O -> C -> I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> I -> C -> B -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
*   J -> D -> P -> I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
(and similar paths starting from J -> P or J -> I, skipping D)

**Attempting a cut set of size 1:**
If I remove any single node, will it disconnect J and F?
*   If I remove E: All Category 1 paths are broken. However, Category 2 paths remain, e.g., J -> I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F. So, removing E alone is not enough.
*   If I remove H: All Category 2 paths (which rely on H to reach G, K, or X) are broken. However, Category 1 paths through E remain, e.g., J -> M -> E -> F. So, removing H alone is not enough.
*   If I remove any other node (M, L, D, P, O, Y, N, C, I, B, G, K, X), there will always be at least one path remaining. For example, removing M leaves J -> I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F. Removing B leaves J -> M -> E -> F.

Since no single node removal disconnects J and F, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

**Attempting a cut set of size 2:**
Let's consider removing nodes E and H.

1.  **Removing E:** This immediately breaks all paths in Category 1, as E is the direct link to F for these paths. This includes paths like J -> M -> E -> F, J -> M -> L -> E -> F, and all paths that go through B to E (e.g., J -> ... -> B -> E -> F).

2.  **After removing E, all remaining paths to F must go through X or K.**
    *   F is connected to X and K.
    *   X is connected to F, K, G.
    *   K is connected to F, X, G.
    *   Therefore, to reach X or K from J, we must reach G. (Note: X and K are connected to each other, but G is the only node connecting them to the rest of the graph from the J-side, without going through H or B which would then lead to H).
    *   Let's trace paths to G: G is connected to H, K, X.
    *   **Removing H:** This breaks all paths that lead to G via H (e.g., J -> ... -> I -> H -> G, J -> ... -> B -> H -> G).
    *   After removing H, G is only connected to K and X.
    *   Are there any paths from J to G that do not use H? No. All paths from J to G must pass through H (e.g., J -> ... -> I -> H -> G or J -> ... -> B -> H -> G).
    *   Since H is removed, G becomes isolated from the J-side of the graph.
    *   Since G is isolated from J, and G is the only way to reach K or X from the J-side (without using H), then no path from J can reach K or X.
    *   Therefore, F is disconnected from J.

Thus, removing nodes E and H successfully disconnects J and F. This is a cut set of size 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

*   All paths from E to I either pass through node H (paths 1-5) OR they pass through node M (paths 6-8).

This means that H and M are crucial "bottleneck" nodes. If I remove H, all paths that go through H are broken. If I remove M, all paths that go through M are broken.

Let's test if a single node removal is sufficient:
*   If I remove H: Paths 1-5 are broken. However, paths 6-8 (E - M - J - I, etc.) still exist. So, removing H alone is not enough.
*   If I remove M: Paths 6-8 are broken. However, paths 1-5 (E - H - I, etc.) still exist. So, removing M alone is not enough.
*   If I remove J: Paths 6-8 are broken. However, paths 1-5 (E - H - I, etc.) still exist. So, removing J alone is not enough.
*   If I remove any other single node (G, F, K, X, L, P, D, C, B, N, O, Y), at least one of the main branches (through H or through M/J) would remain intact. For example, removing G would break paths 2-5, but E-H-I and E-M-J-I would still exist.

Since a single node removal is not sufficient, I need to consider removing two nodes.
Based on my observation that all paths must pass through H or M, I can form a cut set of size 2:
1.  Remove H: This breaks all paths that go through H (paths 1-5).
2.  Then, to break the remaining paths (6-8), I need to remove a node from the E-M-J-I sequence. Removing M would break all of these.
    Thus, removing [H, M] would disconnect E and I.

Alternatively:
1.  Remove H: This breaks all paths that go through H (paths 1-5).
2.  Then, to break the remaining paths (6-8), I need to remove a node from the E-M-J-I sequence. Removing J would break all of these.
    Thus, removing [H, J] would disconnect E and I.

Both [H, M] and [H, J] are cut sets of size 2. Since a single node removal was not sufficient, 2 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, M]

2.  **Identify direct neighbors of target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of E: L, M, F, G, B
    *   Neighbors of I: H, J, C, P
    Any path from E to I must pass through one of E's neighbors and one of I's neighbors. To disconnect E and I, we must remove a set of nodes that severs all connections between E's neighborhood and I's neighborhood.

3.  **Look for node-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):** Finding the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between E and I will give us the minimum size of the cut set.
    Let's try to find three node-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E -> M -> J -> I
        (Intermediate nodes: {M, J})
    *   **Path 2:** E -> G -> H -> I
        (Intermediate nodes: {G, H})
    *   **Path 3:** E -> B -> C -> I
        (Intermediate nodes: {B, C})
    These three paths are node-disjoint (meaning their intermediate nodes {M, J, G, H, B, C} are all distinct). According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect E and I is at least 3.

4.  **Identify candidate cut nodes:** To achieve disconnection, we need to remove at least one node from each of these three node-disjoint paths (excluding E and I).
    *   From Path 1 (E-M-J-I), we could remove M or J.
    *   From Path 2 (E-G-H-I), we could remove G or H.
    *   From Path 3 (E-B-C-I), we could remove B or C.

5.  **Test a cut set of size 3:** Let's try removing {M, G, B}.

    *   **Remove G:** This breaks Path 2 (E-G-H-I) and any other path going through G (e.g., E-F-G-H-I, E-F-K-G-H-I, E-F-X-K-G-H-I).
    *   **Remove B:** This breaks Path 3 (E-B-C-I) and any other path going through B.

6.  **Verify complete disconnection with {M, G, B}:**
    After removing M, G, and B:
    *   **Can E reach H?** H is only connected to G and I. Since G is removed, E cannot reach H.
    *   **Can E reach J?** J is connected to M, P, I. Since M is removed, the paths E-M-J-I and E-L-M-J-I are broken. J is also connected to P, which can be reached from E via B (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-J-I). However, B is also removed, so all paths from E to P (and subsequently to J via P) are broken. Thus, E cannot reach J.
    *   **Can E reach C?** C is connected to B, N, O, I. Since B is removed, all paths from E to C (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-C-I) are broken. Thus, E cannot reach C.

    Since E cannot reach any of I's direct neighbors (H, J, C, P) after removing M, G, and B, E is completely disconnected from I.

    *   F-X-G-H-I-P-D
    *   F-G-H-I-P-D

    *   (F,E) breaks all paths starting through E.
    *   (G,H) breaks all paths starting through G, K, or X, as they all converge on G and must use (G,H) to proceed towards D.

Image name: Tu_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 8
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, I and E, without removing I or E themselves.

1.  **Identify all direct and indirect paths from I to E.**
    Let's list the neighbors of I: H, C, P, J.
    Let's list the neighbors of E: P, L, M, B.

2.  **Analyze paths through common neighbors or critical intermediate nodes.**
    Notice that node P is a common neighbor of both I and E (I-P-E is a direct path).
    Also, many paths from I to E seem to funnel through P. Let's trace them:
    *   **Paths through P:**
        *   I - P - E (Direct path)
        *   I - J - P - E
        *   I - H - G - F - P - E
        *   I - H - G - K - F - P - E
        *   I - H - G - X - F - P - E
        It appears that all paths involving nodes J, D, F, G, K, X from I to E must pass through P. Therefore, removing node P would break all these paths.

3.  **Analyze remaining paths if P is removed.**
    If P is removed, I is now connected to H, C, J. E is connected to L, M, B.
    *   Paths starting with I-J: J is connected to D. D is not connected to E or its remaining neighbors (L, M, B). So, I-J leads to a dead end for E.
    *   Paths starting with I-H: H is connected to G and C.
        *   I - H - G: G is connected to F, K, X. F, K, X are all connected to P, which is now removed. None of F, G, K, X are connected to E or its remaining neighbors (L, M, B). So, I-H-G leads to a dead end for E.
        *   I - H - C: This path leads to the "upper-middle" part of the graph.
    *   Paths starting with I-C: This path also leads to the "upper-middle" part of the graph.

    So, if P is removed, any remaining path from I to E must go through C (from I's side) and B (to E's side). Let's verify this:
    *   I - C - B - E
    *   I - C - N - B - E
    *   I - C - O - B - E
    *   I - C - N - O - B - E
    *   I - C - O - N - B - E
    *   I - C - N - Y - O - B - E
    *   I - C - O - Y - N - B - E
    *   I - H - C - B - E (and all variations through N, O, Y as above)

    All these paths, after P is removed, pass through both C and B. This means that to break all these remaining paths, we must remove either C or B.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set.**
    We've established that removing P is necessary to break one major group of paths. After P is removed, all remaining paths from I to E pass through both C and B. Therefore, we need to remove one more node: either C or B.

    This leads to two possible minimal cut sets of size 2:
    *   [P, C]: Removing P breaks all paths through the lower-left part of the graph. Removing C breaks all paths through the upper-middle part (I-C-...-B-E).
    *   [P, B]: Removing P breaks all paths through the lower-left part of the graph. Removing B breaks all paths through the upper-middle part (I-C-...-B-E).

5.  **Verify if a single node removal is sufficient.**
    *   Removing P: Paths like I-C-B-E would remain.
    *   Removing C: Paths like I-P-E would remain.
    *   Removing B: Paths like I-P-E would remain.
    *   Removing any other single node (H, J, D, F, G, K, X, N, O, Y, L, M) would leave multiple paths intact (e.g., I-P-E and I-C-B-E).
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

Since a single node is not enough, and we found a set of 2 nodes that disconnects I and E, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [P, C] or [P, B]

3.  **Consider a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If G is removed: Paths like E-B-H or E-L-M-J-P-I-H still exist.
    *   If I is removed: Paths like E-G-H or E-B-H still exist.
    *   If B is removed: Paths like E-G-H or E-L-M-J-P-I-H still exist.
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

    *   If G and I are removed: The path E-B-H still exists.
    *   If G and B are removed: The path E-L-M-J-P-I-H still exists.
    *   If I and B are removed: The path E-G-H still exists.

5.  **Consider a cut set of size 3:**
    *   If G, I, and B are removed: H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. No path can reach H from E. This set [G, I, B] successfully disconnects E and H.

    *   Path 1: E - G - H (intermediate node: G)
    *   Path 2: E - B - H (intermediate node: B)
    *   Path 3: E - L - M - J - P - I - H (intermediate nodes: L, M, J, P, I)
    Since these three paths are node-disjoint (except for E and H), Menger's Theorem states that at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E and H.
7.  **Conclusion:** We found a cut set of size 3 ([G, I, B]), and we've established that a minimum of 3 nodes are required. Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 8
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and B.

1.  **Identify direct connections and immediate paths:**
    *   There is a direct edge between H and B: (H, B).
    *   H is connected to G, and G is connected to B: H - G - B.
    *   H is connected to I. From I, there are paths to B. For example: H - I - P - O - C - B.

2.  **Look for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges required to disconnect two nodes, I can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. By Menger's theorem (edge version), this number is equal to the minimum number of edges in an edge cut.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from H to B:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B
        *   Edges used: (H, B)
    *   **Path 2:** H - G - B
        *   Edges used: (H, G), (G, B)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 because it uses different edges.
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - P - O - C - B
        *   Edges used: (H, I), (I, P), (P, O), (O, C), (C, B)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (does not use (H,B)) and Path 2 (does not use (H,G) or (G,B)).

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between H and B, according to Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H from B.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all its incident edges.
    The edges incident to H are:
    *   (H, B)
    *   (H, G)
    *   (H, I)

    If these three edges are removed, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore, no path can exist between H and B. This cut set has 3 edges.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I have identified a minimum of 3 edge-disjoint paths, and I have found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects H from B, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['B', 'H'], ['H', 'I'], ['G', 'H']]}

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (excluding the source and target nodes) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.
6.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:** To break these three paths, we need to remove at least one node from each path's intermediate nodes.

    *   To break Path 3 (E - I - H), we must remove I.
    Therefore, a cut set of size 3 would be {B, I, F} or {B, I, G}.
7.  **Verify the chosen cut set:** Let's choose the cut set [B, I, G].
    *   If B is removed, all paths going through B (e.g., E-B-H, E-B-C-I-H, etc.) are broken.
    *   If I is removed, all paths going through I (e.g., E-I-H, E-B-C-I-H, E-L-M-J-I-H, etc.) are broken.
    *   If G is removed, the path E-F-G-H is broken.
    After removing B, I, and G, the node H has no remaining connections to any other white node (its only neighbors were G, B, and I, all of which are now removed). Therefore, H is completely disconnected from E.

    *   H is directly connected to G, F, B, and I.
    *   Any path from H to O must start by traversing one of these edges.

3.  **Analyze Paths through G:**
    *   H -> G. From G, connections are to F, K, X.
    *   K is connected to G, F, X.
    *   X is connected to G, F, K.
    *   Crucially, to reach the "right" side of the graph (towards O), any path from G (or K, X) must pass through F (via G-F, K-F, or X-F) and then through E (via F-E).
    *   Therefore, all paths originating from H through G eventually pass through F.

4.  **Identify Critical Junctions:** Based on the above, all paths from H to O must pass through one of the following nodes: B, I, or F.
    *   If a path goes H -> B, it continues towards O.
    *   If a path goes H -> I, it continues towards O.
    *   If a path goes H -> F (or H -> G -> F), it continues towards O via F -> E.

5.  **Hypothesize a Cut Set:** If we remove nodes B, I, and F, then:
    *   The direct connection H-B is severed.
    *   The direct connection H-I is severed.
    *   The direct connection H-F is severed.
    *   The paths H-G-F are also severed because F is removed.
    *   With B, I, and F removed, H is only connected to G, K, and X. None of these nodes (G, K, X) have any remaining connections to the part of the graph containing O.
    *   Thus, removing {B, I, F} successfully disconnects H from O. This is a cut set of size 3.

6.  **Verify Minimum Size (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm that 3 is the minimum, I need to find at least 3 node-disjoint paths between H and O (excluding H and O themselves).
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N - O (Intermediate nodes: B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - O (Intermediate nodes: I, C)
    *   **Path 3:** H - F - E - M - J - D - P - O (Intermediate nodes: F, E, M, J, D, P)

    Let's check for node-disjointness:
    *   Path 1 uses {B, N}.
    *   Path 2 uses {I, C}.
    *   Path 3 uses {F, E, M, J, D, P}.
    All intermediate nodes in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, these are three node-disjoint paths.

7.  **Conclusion:** According to Menger's Theorem, if there are `k` node-disjoint paths between two nodes, then at least `k` nodes must be removed to disconnect them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3. As I have already identified a cut set of size 3 ({B, I, F}), this is indeed the minimum.

    *   Edges in P2: `{(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}`
    *   Edges in P3: `{(E, I), (I, B), (B, H)}`
    *   Edges in P4: `{(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)}`

3.  **Verify if these paths are edge-disjoint.**
    *   P1 uses only `(E, H)`.
    *   P2 uses `(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)`. None of these edges are in P1.
    *   P3 uses `(E, I), (I, B), (B, H)`. None of these edges are in P1 or P2.
    *   P4 uses `(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`. None of these edges are in P1, P2, or P3.
    Since all four paths use entirely different sets of edges, they are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem.**
    Menger's Theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. Since we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 4.

5.  **Find a cut set of 4 edges.**
    To disconnect H from E, we can remove all edges incident to H. The edges connected to H are:
    *   `(H, E)` (which is the same as `(E, H)`)
    *   `(H, G)`
    *   `(H, B)`
    *   `(H, I)`
    If we remove these 4 edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from E. This set of edges successfully disconnects E and H.

6.  **Conclusion.**
    We have identified 4 edge-disjoint paths, establishing a lower bound of 4 for the cut size. We have also found a cut set of 4 edges that successfully disconnects E and H. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']]}

3.  **Identify Edge-Disjoint Paths:** I will systematically search for paths from E to J that do not share any common edges.
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        *   Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - P - J
        *   Edges: {(E, P), (P, J)}

    *   **Path 3:** E - B - C - I - J
        *   Edges: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J)}
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2. It does not use (E, M), (M, J), (E, P), or (P, J).

    *   Edges in Path 1: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   Edges in Path 2: {(E, P), (P, J)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J)}
    *   There are no common edges among these three sets. Thus, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J.
5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.
6.  **Find a Minimal Cut Set:** Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E and J. A straightforward approach is to cut all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that lead towards the other.

    *   If (E, P) is removed, E can no longer reach P.
    *   If (E, B) is removed, E can no longer reach B.
    *   After removing these three edges, E is only connected to L. Node L is only connected to E and M. Since (E, M) is removed, E cannot reach M via L either. Therefore, E is completely isolated from any path leading to J.
8.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects E and J. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   M also connects to K, which can lead to B.
    *   M also connects to L, which only connects to E.

    *   **Group 1: Paths through E.** Any path from M that starts M-E, or M-L-E, falls into this group. Since L is only connected to E, all paths starting M-L must eventually pass through E to reach B. Therefore, all paths from M that do not start with M-K must pass through E.
    *   **Group 2: Paths through K.** These paths start M-K. From K, paths can go to X or F, then to G, then to H, and finally to B. Examples: M-K-X-G-H-B, M-K-F-G-H-B.

    *   **To break Group 2 paths (through K):** After removing E, the only remaining paths from M to B are those starting M-K. These paths are M-K-X-G-H-B and M-K-F-G-H-B. All these paths pass through K. Therefore, removing K will break all remaining paths from M to B.
    *   Thus, removing nodes E and K will disconnect M from B.

    *   If K is also removed: M is now only connected to L. Since E is also removed, L is disconnected from the rest of the graph. Therefore, M is completely isolated from B.

    *   Other valid cut sets of size 2 exist, such as [E, G] or [E, H], as G and H are also critical nodes in the M-K-...-B path. However, any one of these is sufficient for the answer.

        Edges: {(H,F), (F,E), (E,I), (I,B)}

2.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths.**
    Let's list the edges for each path and check for overlaps:
    *   P1: {(H,B)}
    *   P2: {(H,E), (E,B)}
    *   P3: {(H,I), (I,C), (C,B)}
    *   P4: {(H,F), (F,E), (E,I), (I,B)}

    All edges listed above are distinct. For example, (H,B) is only in P1. (H,E) and (E,B) are only in P2. (H,I), (I,C), (C,B) are only in P3. (H,F), (F,E), (E,I), (I,B) are only in P4.
    Since we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 4.

3.  **Find a cut set of 4 edges and verify its effectiveness.**
    Based on the edge-disjoint paths, we can construct a cut set by taking one edge from each path. Let's choose the following edges:
    Cut Set: [(H,B), (H,E), (H,I), (F,E)]

    Now, let's verify that removing these 4 edges completely disconnects H from B:
    *   Removing (H,B) breaks the direct path P1.
    *   Removing (H,E) breaks path P2 (H-E-B) and any other path starting H-E-....
    *   Removing (H,I) breaks path P3 (H-I-C-B) and any other path starting H-I-....
    *   Removing (F,E) breaks path P4 (H-F-E-I-B) and any other path from H that goes through F then E.

    Let's trace all possible paths from H after removing these edges:
    *   H's remaining direct connections are to G and F.
    *   **From H to G:** H - G. G is connected to X, K, F, H. None of these nodes (X, K, F) can lead to B without passing through E or I.
        *   G -> F. But (F,E) is removed, so F cannot reach E. F's other connections (X, K, G, H) are not connected to B.
    *   **From H to F:** H - F. F is connected to X, K, G, H, E. But (F,E) is removed, so F cannot reach E. F's other connections (X, K, G, H) are not connected to B.

    Since H cannot reach E or I (because (H,E) and (H,I) are removed, and paths through F or G to E are blocked by (F,E)), and B is only connected to H, E, I, C, N, there is no way for H to reach B. Therefore, H and B are completely disconnected.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    We found 4 edge-disjoint paths, meaning at least 4 edges must be removed. We also found a cut set of 4 edges that successfully disconnects H from B. Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H,B), (H,E), (H,I), (F,E)]

The target nodes are N and G. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect N from G.

First, I identify the direct neighbors of the target nodes:
Neighbors of N: Y, O, B
Neighbors of G: K, X, H

Any path from N to G must pass through one of N's neighbors, and eventually through one of G's neighbors.

Let's consider the edges directly connected to G: (K, G), (X, G), (H, G). If I remove all three of these edges, G would be isolated, and thus disconnected from N. This would be a cut set of size 3. I need to check if a smaller cut set exists.

Let's analyze the paths from N to G. I can categorize paths based on which neighbor of G they reach:

**Category 1: Paths to G via H**
*   N - B - H - G
*   N - B - C - I - H - G
*   N - Y - O - P - I - H - G
*   N - O - P - I - H - G
*   N - O - C - B - H - G
All these paths share the edge (H, G) as their final segment to reach G through H. Removing (H, G) would cut all paths in this category.

**Category 2: Paths to G via K or X**
To reach K or X from N, all paths must eventually pass through node F, as F is the only node connected to both K and X (other than G itself).
Let's trace paths from N to F:
*   N - B - E - F
*   N - Y - O - P - I - C - B - E - F
*   N - O - P - I - C - B - E - F
*   N - O - C - B - E - F
All these paths from N to F share the edge (E, F) as a critical link to reach F from the "left" side of the graph (N's side).
Therefore, any path from N to G that goes through K or X must pass through F, and to reach F from N, it must pass through E. This means the edge (E, F) is a bottleneck for all paths leading to K or X from N.

**Proposed Cut Set:**
Based on the analysis, if I remove the edge (H, G), all paths from N to G that go through H are broken.
If I then remove the edge (E, F), all paths from N to G that go through K or X are broken, because they all rely on the connection from E to F to reach K or X.

Let's verify this cut set: **[(H, G), (E, F)]**
1.  **Removing (H, G):** This severs all paths that use H to reach G.
2.  **Removing (E, F):** This severs all paths that use E to reach F. Since F is the only node connecting to K and X (other than G), and all paths from N to F must pass through E, removing (E, F) effectively disconnects K and X from N's side of the graph. Consequently, no paths can reach G via K or X from N.

With both (H, G) and (E, F) removed, all possible paths from N to G are broken. This cut set has a size of 2.

**Checking for a smaller cut set (size 1):**
If I remove any single edge, will N and G be disconnected?
*   If I remove (H, G), paths like N - B - E - F - K - G still exist.
*   If I remove (E, F), paths like N - B - H - G still exist.
*   If I remove (K, G), paths like N - B - H - G and N - B - E - F - X - G still exist.
*   If I remove (X, G), paths like N - B - H - G and N - B - E - F - K - G still exist.
*   If I remove (B, H), paths like N - Y - O - P - I - H - G and N - B - E - F - K - G still exist.
*   And so on for any other single edge.
Since removing any single edge leaves at least one path between N and G, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, G), (E, F)]

    *   This establishes an upper bound of 4 for the minimum cut.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1 (P1): G - H - I**

    Since removing any single edge does not disconnect L and J, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.
